# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Or you can fork this repository and set your own key words in `main.py`:

Last update: 2026-10-15

👍Thanks to [zezhishao/DailyArXiv](https://github.com/zezhishao/DailyArXiv) and [Cool Paper](https://papers.cool).

| **[PixelMoE: Scaling Pixel-Space Diffusion with LLM-Style Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18857v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18857v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pixel-space diffusion models provide an appealing alternative to latent-space diffusion models by directly modeling raw images without relying on a learned tokenizer. However, scaling pixel-space models has predominantly followed dense strategies, tightly coupling model capacity with active computation. In this work, we show that standard LLM-style Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures can be effectively transferred to pixel-space diffusion. Building on this finding, we introduce PixelMoE, a pixel-space generative framework that sparsely scales model capacity with standard LLM-style MoE, without diffusion-specific routing mechanisms or auxiliary routing objectives. Specifically, PixelMoE combines standard Token-Choice (TC) routing with fine-grained routed experts for input-adaptive sparse computation, a shared expert for capturing common representations, and a bias-based load-balancing strategy. To complement sparse capacity scaling, PixelMoE further introduces Pixel-Space Hierarchical Flow Matching, which varies spatial resolution along the denoising trajectory from global structure formation to fine-grained detail refinement. At comparable active parameter counts, PixelMoE consistently outperforms its dense counterparts across model scales, demonstrating effective sparse capacity scaling in pixel space. On class-conditional ImageNet-1K, PixelMoE achieves FID scores of 1.47 at 256x256 and 1.53 at 512x512 without representation alignment or perceptual losses, comparing favorably with leading pixel-space and latent-space generative models. Code and models will be released soon.</p></details> |  |
| **[Back-Translation Still Matters: Leveraging Monolingual Data for Low-Resource Machine Translation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18818v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18818v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-trained Large Language Model (LLMs) are effective on many tasks in zero-shot and few-shot settings. For machine translation of low-resource languages, the obvious options are few-shot prompting and fine-tuning on a small parallel set. We ask: what if moderate amounts of low-resource monolingual data are also available? We argue that, rather than continued pretraining, a more effective use of this data is the pre-LLM era technique of back-translation (BT). The current fine-tuned LLM translates monolingual sentences in each language, and the resulting pairs train it in the reverse direction. Updates on the parallel set are interleaved for stable learning. With 1K parallel pairs on Odia and Swahili, BT beats continued pretraining into the low-resource language by 7.7 to 16.4 XCOMET-XXL points in domain. With beam-decoded intermediate translations, it also beats a model with 10 time more parallel data. In-context examples retrieved from the parallel data further improve BT. We also analyze the source, quality and amount of monolingual text. Across five low-resource languages, with Hindi as a high-resource control, and two 3B backbones, BT improves translation into the low-resource language by 16 to 173% XCOMET-XXL over parallel-only fine-tuning. Blind human evaluation on Odia and Sanskrit agrees with the automatic metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where Do LLM Ensemble Gains Come From? Decomposing Candidate Value, Diversity, and Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18786v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18786v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM ensemble gains are often measured against a single baseline, but the value attributed to additional candidates can change with the comparison used. We study an LLM aggregator under four matched conditions: Direct (no candidates), Single-slot (one candidate in a multi-slot interface), Duplicate (one candidate repeated), and Multi-candidate (separately generated candidates). Under either non-diverse control, overall candidate gain (CIV) splits algebraically into an additional-candidate contrast (DSV) and a candidate-context contrast (CCV); the split, but not CIV, depends on the chosen control. On MultiHopRAG model diversity (k=2), the repetition gap Rep=-8.33 pp [-13.25, -3.75] quantifies the difference between gains measured against Duplicate and Single-slot. On a matched FRAMES stronger-panel extension (n=200, k=3, aggregator output limit 4096), paired Delta Rep=+9.67 pp [+6.00, +13.50]; within panels, Rep changes sign (original -6.33; stronger +3.33). In FRAMES evidence aggregation, candidate-context contrasts remain strongly negative under both non-diverse controls. Our results show why ensemble evaluations should state the reference control before attributing gains to additional candidates.</p></details> | 14 pages, 6 figures |
| **[From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26277v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.26277v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.</p></details> | <details><summary>Dianj...</summary><p>Dianjing Fan and Yao Li equally contributed to this work. 7 pages, 1 figure</p></details> |
| **[Looking Complete Is Not Being Grounded: Evaluating LLM Sitreps Against Human-Authored Situation Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18762v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18762v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluation for automatic crisis reporting should measure what practitioners need, not what is convenient to compare. Through LLM-assisted, author-approved content analysis, we derive a 15-slot information schema from professional humanitarian situation reports using qualitative content analysis, then evaluate 121 human and 261 LLM-generated reports across five disasters and three hazard types. Under google/gemini-3.7-flash on Cyclone Idai, the best machine arm is nominally above the empirical human ceiling (0.827 vs. 0.821), but the difference is statistically indistinguishable from zero (95\% bootstrap CI $[-.049,.059]$; permutation $p=.852$), and the ordering does not replicate under openai/gpt-5.6-luna. On a blind 100-item Idai sample, the author agrees with the Gemini judge at 0.910 accuracy and unweighted Cohen's $κ=.799$. All four judges clear the plan's raw-agreement criterion of .7; only Gemini reaches .7 on chance-corrected $κ$. Across the three primary events, machine reports reproduce 5.4-11.0% of same-day official figures. Across those same events, raw exact-number matching links 14.3-19.6% of official figures and 85.4-90.1% of machine figures to the social stream; manual audit confirms 45/50 declared absences, 4/25 official matches, and 24/25 machine matches semantically. The issuing-provenance gap is positive in all 12 observed event-judge cells: report date, organisation, series, and authority were not supplied at professional-report coverage in any cell. On the three primary events, ROUGE-L is uninformative, BERTScore is negatively associated with completeness after multiplicity correction, and a generic LLM judge changes sign.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at LT4CPR Workshop @ AACL-IJCNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Energy-Aware Scheduling for Serverless LLM Serving on Shared GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30391v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.30391v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM inference becomes a major cloud workload, its growing energy footprint makes cluster-wide energy optimization increasingly important. Serverless LLM serving helps platforms absorb traffic volatility by elastically sharing GPU resources across models, but this sharing also makes energy optimization difficult. Multiple co-resident models run under one device-wide operating point, while their resource demands and latency slack change across execution phases and load conditions. As a result, minimizing energy requires coordinated scheduling across request placement, runtime resource adaptation, and workload consolidation. We present Festina, a profiling-guided, power-aware control plane to minimize cluster-wide energy for serverless LLM serving. Unlike common global-local schedulers that focus on throughput or tail latency, Festina makes energy-first decisions by jointly coordinating request placement, SM partitioning, and GPU operating points under TTFT/TBT SLOs. In our system, a lightweight global scheduler performs fast, SLO-safe, energy-aware placement using constant-time lookups from offline profiles and GPU state summaries. On each GPU, a phase-aware local scheduler continuously adapts task batching and compute resources to minimize power consumption. Festina further performs energy-aware workload consolidation to reduce GPUs' static power consumption via SLO-aware migration. Comparison with four SOTA LLM serving systems and one DVFS-augmented system demonstrates that Festina reduces energy consumption by up to 56% while maintaining parity in SLO attainment (within a 2% margin)</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages body and 4 pages appendix, 19 pages total. Accepted to the 2026 ACM SIGOPS Annual Technical Conference (SIGOPS ATC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[FinTradeBench: A Financial Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19225v6)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.19225v6) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial analysis requires reasoning over heterogeneous signals, including company fundamentals from regulatory filings and trading signals derived from market prices. However, existing financial benchmarks focus almost exclusively on regulatory filings, neglecting market dynamics and cross-signal interactions. We introduce FinTradeBench, a benchmark of 1,400 questions covering NASDAQ-100 stocks over a ten-year window. To holistically evaluate financial reasoning, FinTradeBench includes questions in three reasoning categories: fundamentals-focused, trading-signal-focused, and hybrid questions that require cross-signal reasoning. Our benchmark starts with 150 expert-authored seed questions, which are then scaled to 1400. To ensure reliable evaluation, we create a Core400 subset in which every item is expert-reviewed. In zero-shot (No-RAG) response generation, we observe that all models perform substantially worse on trading-focused questions compared to fundamental-focused questions. To identify failure causes, we evaluate LLMs under controlled settings including retrieval-based baselines, tool-assisted computation, oracle conditions, and time-series-specialized pipelines. LLMs' capabilities to do computation and evidence selection contribute most to reasoning failures. Additionally, retrieval significantly improves trading-signal reasoning but does not improve fundamental reasoning, where identifying relevant signals remains the dominant failure mode. Finally, rather than relying on a small, fine-tuned model for time-series reasoning, it is better to summarize the time-series data and leverage LLMs' reasoning capabilities.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages main text, 36 pages total (including references and appendix). 9 figures, 31 tables. Preprint under review. Code and data will be made available upon publication</p></details> |
| **[LLM-as-Jev: LLMs Are Already Jev-Style Decision Models -- When and How to Fine-Tune Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02076v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.02076v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Jev-style decision models have gained surging traction by making decision-making dramatically faster and more efficient, returning categorical probability distributions over predefined options without generating free-form text. However, because Jev's architecture and training methodologies remain proprietary, how to replicate these capabilities effectively remains an open question. In this work, we investigate the extent to which general-purpose LLMs already possess this capability out of the box, and when fine-tuning is actually necessary. We present LLM-as-Jev, an architecture-preserving framework offering both a training-free inference recipe and a fine-tuning objective with KL-divergence anchors. Across Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3-0.6B, we find that modern LLMs are inherently effective decision models: off the shelf, frozen models match or exceed existing training-free readouts on the same 4B backbone, scale to arbitrary option counts, and natively handle multimodal decisions. They also seamlessly inherit the backbone's thinking mode: while Jev was introduced as a System 1 model, "Thinking Jev" leverages budgeted test-time reasoning to boost accuracy on challenging tasks without training. Fine-tuning provides targeted gains for smaller models and complex routing, while our KL anchors prevent behavioral degradation in general text generation.</p></details> |  |
| **[OracleProto: A Reproducible Framework for Benchmarking LLM Forecasting via Knowledge Cutoffs and Temporal Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03762v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.03762v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Forecasting is a composite capability encompassing information gathering, evidence integration, situational assessment, and decision-making. Large language models (LLMs) combine reasoning and tool use, offering new avenues for automated forecasting and decision support. Yet LLM forecasting evaluation faces a structural challenge: live benchmarks support prospective assessment, but limited question reuse and changing information conditions make results difficult to reproduce and compare; retrospective benchmarks offer reproducibility but risk information leakage. We introduce OracleProto, a reproducible framework for evaluating LLM forecasting in a controlled, temporally masked environment. Through model knowledge cutoff constraints and temporal masking, OracleProto constructs a temporal sandbox for real-world web retrieval, audits information boundaries through leakage detection, and reconstructs resolved events as forecasting instances. Discrete answer normalization and hierarchical scoring enable unified evaluation. We evaluate 27 model-configuration pairs on 300 FutureX-Past-derived questions over 24,300 prediction trials, assessing forecasting quality, sampling stability, and cost efficiency under controlled information boundaries. An audit finds only 1.37% residual leakage in retrieval results after temporal masking and leakage filtering. OracleProto retains resolved forecasting tasks as reusable evaluation resources under explicit temporal boundaries, supporting continual accumulation of forecasting datasets and systematic evaluation of forecasting capabilities. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OracleProto-2E3A/.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Users See Is Not What Models Read: Split-View PDFs in Document-to-LLM Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15020v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.15020v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document-to-LLM applications typically read uploaded PDFs by first translating them into text through an extraction layer whose output is not ordinarily exposed to users. We show that this layer enables split-view PDFs: one document can have two semantic views before model reasoning. By mining specification-permitted or implementation-tolerated representation gaps at the PDF render/extract boundary, we instantiate 25 extraction gaps (EG) in which extractors return attacker-controlled or extractor-dependent text while the rendered page shows benign or different content. The gaps form four families: semantic overrides, hidden semantic injection, reading-order splits, and font-decoding splits, and 14 gaps have no exact path/mechanism-level match in prior PDF-to-LLM attacks. We evaluate these gaps on 16 PDF processing stacks and 7 commercial LLM services. Each gap causes render-extract divergence on at least one stack. Under a gap-level exposure criterion, every evaluated service exposes at least one gap, with 12/25 to 21/25 exposed gaps. Exposure is driven mainly by the ingestion stack -- not model identity alone. We further show that tested safety filters cover only selected hidden-text constructions. To support triage, we develop a static screening scanner whose rules trigger on all 25 benchmark gaps, and discuss dual-view consistency as a longer-term defense direction.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (S&P 2027)</p></details> |
| **[Can an Agent Learn to Predict Its Own Failure? Outcome-Grounded Confidence Calibration in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18614v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18614v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Controlling an acting LLM agent means deciding, at each step of a long-horizon task, whether to trust its next action, abstain, or hand off to a human. That decision needs a per-step confidence signal predicting whether the current rollout will eventually succeed. The agent's own self-reported verbalized confidence is the natural candidate, but existing reinforcement learning methods calibrate it against the correctness of a decision at the current step. We propose a method to calibrate it against the eventual outcome of a multi-step episode: under GRPO, the group of rollouts for a task supplies success labels, and a proper scoring rule scores each step's confidence against the realized outcome. In offline RL on recorded trajectories of a Qwen2.5-7B agent in ALFWorld, TextWorld, and WebShop environments, the AUROC of step confidence against episode outcome rises from near chance to 0.68-0.85, and the top-10% of steps the agent is most confident about succeed ~4.3x more often than average. In online RL on the live ALFWorld agent, four calibration rewards improve early-step AUROC, expected calibration error goes from 0.42 to below 0.12, and selective execution improves over both the untrained agent and a format-only RL control, without degrading task success. We also introduce an evaluation protocol that separates episode depth, task difficulty, and attempt-level foresight, which shows where the gain lies: the trained agent recognizes from its first step which tasks it will fail, while telling a succeeding attempt from a failing one at the same task is harder, with some early-window signal emerging on TextWorld.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking Projector Training in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18598v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18598v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal LLMs are typically trained by first aligning a vision encoder with a language model via projector training on large image-caption datasets, followed by joint training of the full multimodal model. Despite its widespread use, the role of the projector training stage remains poorly understood. We find that (i) using only $10$ to $20\%$ of the data for training the projector already recovers most downstream performance gains, and (ii) projectors trained on different datasets can be linearly interpolated while largely preserving performance. These results suggest that projector training mainly performs a coarse alignment, which many different projectors achieve equally well. Motivated by these findings, we propose PORTAL, a training-free projector initialization that requires neither paired image-caption data nor gradient-based optimization, but only per-modality summary statistics. PORTAL computes an optimal-transport-based map between Gaussian approximations of the vision and language feature distributions on a shared principal subspace, so that the projector training stage can be skipped entirely. Without any paired data, PORTAL matches the standard pipeline trained on 558k image-caption pairs across six LLM backbones, two vision encoders, and 16 benchmarks. It outperforms that pipeline in $3$ of $7$ (vision encoder, LLM) settings and reaches an average $98\%$ of its score across the other four.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 camera-ready</p></details> |
| **[Compare to Correct: Pairwise-Guided Logit Adjustment for LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18583v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18583v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sampling-based test-time scaling enhances LLM reasoning by aggregating multiple reasoning paths for the same query. Existing methods use answer frequencies or path confidence to compute candidate scores, which do not explicitly characterize comparisons between competing answers. Inspired by the Condorcet principle in social choice theory, we introduce pairwise confidence comparisons between paths supporting different answers to capture answer-level comparative advantage. We combine this comparative evidence with voting support through a KL-regularized objective. Theoretically, we show that this comparative-advantage is equivalent to the adjustment to the traditional voting logits. In this way, our proposed Pairwise-Guided Logit Adjustment (PGLA) framework allows comparative evidence to compensate for weaker voting support and correct voting-based ranking errors. Experiments on 10 challenging benchmarks including mathematical reasoning, knowledge reasoning, and code generation with 7 models ranging from 1.7B to 32B parameters speak to the effectiveness of this plug-and-play term. Additional evaluations with external reward scores and online sampling further demonstrate its applicability.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM Agents for Automated Post-Training via Structured Recipe Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18572v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18572v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated post-training transforms pre-trained large language models (LLMs) into models capable of handling diverse domains by automatically constructing and optimizing a recipe of coupled decisions over data sources, data processing, cross-domain composition, and training configuration under a limited experimental budget. Existing automation either searches predefined recipe spaces that enable systematic optimization but remain fixed rather than adapting their available choices to the target tasks, or uses open-ended agents that discover task-specific choices but leave them implicit in trial-level edits, limiting their systematic reuse and recombination across trials. To fill this gap, we introduce optimization over a task-adapted recipe space, a novel paradigm that can adapt the recipe space itself to the target tasks while representing its choices explicitly for systematic reuse, recombination, and joint optimization. We instantiate this paradigm with Structured Recipe Optimization (SRO), comprising an LLM-based Recipe-Space Designer that constructs the task-adapted recipe space and a Multi-Domain Recipe Optimizer that uses structured trial evidence to guide LLM candidate construction and multi-objective Bayesian trial selection. Extensive experiments show that SRO achieves the highest average score in all evaluated target-model and agent-LLM combinations. In joint post-training across three capability domains and six benchmarks, SRO reaches 41.1 versus 15.4 for the strongest baseline on Qwen3-1.7B with GPT-5.4, and 53.8 versus 40.8 on Qwen3-4B with DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Our code is available at https://github.com/RuohLiuq/SRO.</p></details> |  |
| **[Revisiting Cauchy-Schwarz Norm Bounds for SDC Detection in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18557v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18557v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) execute billions of floating-point operations per output token across many hardware accelerators. At this scale, hardware-induced faults can bypass system-level detection mechanisms and cause Silent Data Corruption (SDC). Algorithm-Based Fault Tolerance (ABFT) is the state-of-the-art technique for protecting matrix multiplication from such faults, but it incurs non-negligible computational overhead and requires thresholds to distinguish hardware-induced errors from floating-point roundoff. In this paper, we revisit Cauchy--Schwarz Norm Bounds (CSNB) as a low-overhead alternative for detecting SDC during LLM inference. We compare CSNB against ABFT through controlled fault injection and evaluate the runtime overhead of both verification and threshold determination across multiple model sizes, batch sizes, and generation lengths. Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated dense linear layers while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting dense linear layers in LLM inference, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Spectra to Joint Schedules in LLM Pre-training: 3+3(+2) Scaling-Law Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40148v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.40148v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Power-law learning curves are often treated as fixed properties of a model and its data, although learning-rate and batch-size schedules can change the observed loss. We study this dependence in noisy online SGD with linear random features. Conditional on the representation, an exact Volterra equation separates two response components: a forcing term that propagates unresolved target error and a memory kernel that propagates stochastic-error injections. We prove that either component follows a power law if and only if its cumulative weighted spectral mass has the corresponding low-spectrum scaling; individual eigenvalues and target coefficients need not obey coordinatewise power laws. Under a joint schedule, intrinsic time $T_t=\sum_{s<t}η_s$ controls optimization progress, while $r_t=B_t/η_t$ controls noise injection. Their interaction yields sharp conditions under which a schedule preserves, changes, or destroys the clean power law, together with a memory ceiling on noise reduction. The power-law random-feature model realizes this mechanism in $3+3(+2)$ propagation regimes with phase-dependent compute rates. Controlled nanoGPT experiments show that (1) learning-rate and batch-size schedules with matched $B/η$ paths are nearly equivalent in intrinsic time, (2) a forcing-memory surrogate accurately predicts loss across schedules, and (3) its fitted exponents across real-world datasets identify the regime of LLMs in $3+3(+2)$ map. The source code for reproducing our experiments is available on https://github.com/yichenblue/spectra-to-schedules-in-pretraining.</p></details> |  |
| **[Lost in Implicit Routing: How LLM Agents Fail to Load Relevant Skills at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18522v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18522v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reusable skills let LLM agents load task-specific instructions on demand, but they make execution depend on implicit routing over skill metadata. We test whether this design scales by holding SkillsBench tasks and their golden skills fixed while adding irrelevant skills. We find skill routing collapse: agents increasingly load fewer or no skills, even though the relevant skills remain available. With 1,000 irrelevant skills, loaded-skill recall falls from 73.6% to 27.4% and the zero-load rate rises from 9.3% to 51.6%, whereas explicit selection from the same library retains 73.9% recall. Loading other skills, including plausible alternatives to golden skills, is too rare to explain this decline. Masking only the descriptions of irrelevant skills largely restores skill loading, yet with the metadata intact, explicitly asking agents to plan skill use still yields lower recall than preselection. These findings point to semantic interference from descriptions and instruction interference when skill choice competes with task execution. We also evaluate explicit preselection as a practical mitigation, including its coverage and latency trade-offs. Our analysis informs skill protocols and evaluation for agents with growing skill libraries.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, including references and appendices. Code: https://github.com/jinlinll/lost-in-implicit-routing</p></details> |
| **[A Global View of How LLMs Value Human Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18515v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18515v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as personal assistants; however, little is known about how they capture national-level preferences on a global scale. Given that "time" is a universal and representative concept, we examine LLMs' time preferences by varying only users' country and measuring the compensation LLMs deem appropriate for a 60-minute appointment delay. We then compare LLMs' responses with GDP per capita on Purchasing Power Parity (GDP_PPP), Mean Income, and a literature-based index (Patience Score). Our results across 193 countries and 5 LLMs demonstrate that people's time across the world is evaluated in noticeably different ways among LLMs, as well as varying levels of robustness under prompt variations. LLMs consistently recommend the highest compensation for Europe, but mostly the lowest for Africa. Based on GDP_PPP, LLMs underestimate the value of time for users living in Asia and South America. These findings indicate that geographical patterns are embedded in LLMs and may translate in different treatment in agentic settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Shared Latent Structure of LLM Psychometric Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05080v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.05080v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human psychometric questionnaires are increasingly used to characterize large language models (LLMs), but it remains unclear what exactly their responses reflect. To answer this question we analyze responses from 50 LLMs across 45 questionnaires spanning multiple psychological domains using psychometric methodology. We found a dominant principal component explaining 47.1% of variance across standardized, response-derived questionnaire-level primary scores, revealing substantial shared structure across instruments. We investigate its meaning through semantic analysis, respondent framing, and human annotations. Semantic analysis suggests that the component partly reflects experiential self-attribution: how readily models endorse statements describing subjective states and bodily experiences. A model score emphasizing items whose disagreement decreases under human simulation correlates strongly with the component ($r=0.864$). Human ratings of 289 matched items show that inner-life content is positively associated, and social desirability negatively associated, with item-component correlations after mutual adjustment and controls for other semantic properties. Together, six rated properties explain 22.1% of variance in Fisher-transformed item-component correlations, supporting a partial interpretation involving both experiential self-attribution and socially desirable responding. These findings suggest that LLM psychometric profiles share response tendencies that require interpretation beyond the human traits their questionnaires were designed to measure.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Low Can You Go: Fine-Tuning LLMs with Bayesian Optimization (LlMBO)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18504v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18504v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recently, Morris et al. introduced TinyLoRA, a strategy for LLM fine-tuning which introduces arbitrarily few parameters, and demonstrated accuracy improvements using less than $10$ parameters rather than the ${\gtrsim 10,000}$ typically demanded by LoRA-based methods. | **[Adaptive Co-Serving LLM Watermarking on Modern Inference Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03955v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.03955v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) watermarking is important for ownership verification and intellectual property protection. | **[Kairos: Transactional Security Enforcement at the System Boundary for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18457v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18457v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are becoming an integral part of the software development lifecycle, and the capabilities granted to them allow an agent to act with the user's authority on any resource it can access. | **[An Optical KV Cache Network for Disaggregated LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18444v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18444v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Disaggregated LLM serving moves each request's KV cache from a prefill GPU to a decode GPU before the response begins, and a coding-agent turn often moves the KV of its whole long context. | **[How Well Do LLMs Perform in Peer Review? | **[PDAGENT-BENCH: Characterizing, Grounding, and Architecting LLM/VLM Agents for VLSI Physical Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17253v7)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.17253v7) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models and vision-language models have shown remarkable success in the front-end design of Very Large-Scale Integrated Circuits, yet their capabilities for VLSI physical design remain significantly underexplored. The primary cause is the lack of standardized benchmarks for evaluating agentic physical design workflows that require high-dimensional, multi-stage optimization under strict design constraints, coordinated interaction with diverse Electronic Design Automation tools, and iterative refinement. This work introduces PDAGENT-BENCH, a comprehensive and multi-dimensional benchmark for evaluating LLM/VLM-based agents across the physical design stack. PDAGENT-BENCH integrates both task-level assessment and workflow-level execution. The benchmark suite contains 353 curated problems that combine conceptual questions with real-world industrial artifacts, with expert-validated references and executable solutions. In addition, the benchmark provides a unified, human-aligned agentic physical design workflow framework that enables closed-loop evaluation of holistic physical design in realistic EDA environments. Experiments on 11 state-of-the-art models reveal that while modern LLMs/VLMs perform competitively on conceptual tasks, they remain substantially limited in tool-centric execution (e.g., 42.2% on Innovus script generation) and long-horizon, multi-stage reasoning. Our studies further show that human-skill-enhanced agentic workflows significantly improve end-to-end physical design performance. PDAGENT-BENCH establishes a standardized, reproducible, and realistic evaluation framework for advancing LLM/VLM-driven holistic physical design automation. To ensure full reproducibility, we release \textsc{PDAgent-Bench} at https://github.com/fenggev56/PDAGENT-BENCH.</p></details> |  |
| **[CLIOPATRA: Extracting Private Information from LLM Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09781v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.09781v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The widespread adoption of AI assistants has encouraged the development of privacy-aware platforms designed to extract insights from real-world usage. Their privacy protections primarily rely on layering multiple heuristic techniques, such as PII redaction, clustering, aggregation, and LLM-based privacy auditing. In this paper, we put their privacy claims to the test by presenting CLIOPATRA, the first attack against "privacy-preserving" LLM-based insights systems. Our attack involves an adversary that carefully designs and inserts malicious chats into the system to break multiple layers of protections and induce the leakage of sensitive information from a target user's chat. We evaluate CLIOPATRA on one such platform, Anthropic's Clio (now called "Anthropic Insights"), and target synthetically generated medical chats to show that an adversary can successfully and confidently (with nearly 100% precision) extract the medical history contained in these chats in up to 65% of cases. We also show that CLIOPATRA can stealthily extract information by obfuscating the private information in the generated insights. Finally, we demonstrate that existing ad hoc mitigations, such as LLM-based privacy auditing, are unreliable and fail to detect major leaks. Overall, our findings indicate that, even when layered, current heuristic protections are insufficient to adequately protect user data, and that prompt injection has been an understudied risk in LLM-based insight systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in the Proceedings of the 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026). Please cite the NeurIPS version</p></details> |
| **[High-Throughput LLM-Based Compression and What It Takes: A Systems Study of Inference Engines and Entropy Coding Optimizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17586v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17586v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are attractive text compressors because next-token probabilities can be converted into code lengths. This paper examines systems optimizations for LLM-based compression, including multistream pipelined arithmetic coding and target-interval capture, and evaluates their trade-offs across inference engines, configurations and datasets. We evaluate LLM-based text compression on three different inference engines: Transformers, vLLM, and TensorRT-LLM. Notably, we obtain a speedup of 18.84x compared to prior work's Transformers implementation and a maximum recorded inference speed of 181 kilobytes per second at a compression ratio of 7.17, when running on an NVIDIA A100.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 2 figures. | **[Decision Models vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges: Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29769v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.29769v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM judges now score outputs against rubrics, in benchmarks and as training rewards. Decision models return probabilities over permitted answers with a confidence score but no free text. Two such models are Jev and an open reproduction, Perplexity's Decider. We compare each with three flash-tier LLM judges on nine panels from seven benchmarks with human judgments, giving every judge identical criterion texts. The LLM judges run in two setups, reading a whole rubric at once or one criterion at a time. Our findings suggest both decision models can often stand in for them. The LLM judges cost 16 to 357 times as much and take 5 to 350 times as long, yet in both setups each decision model trails them significantly in at most 6 of 27 comparisons. The two kinds of judge err alike, agreeing more with one another than with the human-annotated judgment labels on ordinal criteria. Over 85% of flash-tier LLM verdicts on either decision model's most confident errors repeat them, where independent errors would give about half. Each decision model's confidence ranks its errors on most ordinal panels, so each is a natural first stage for a cascade deferring uncertain verdicts to an LLM judge. Yet with flash-tier fallbacks, such cascades gained a mere 2.7 points at best over the strongest single judge, even with oracle thresholds, suggesting they mainly save cost. A cascade needs a fallback that is right where its first stage errs, but the LLM judges are mostly wrong in the same places as Jev, and the Decider is already the strongest single judge on most panels. Even each vendor's higher-tier model repeats those errors, and as a fallback it lifted no cascade more than 1.7 points above both tiers' strongest single judge. Holding across setups, effort levels, and tiers, these findings suggest designing future decision models afresh to get right what LLM judges get wrong, and vice versa.</p></details> | <details><summary>58 pa...</summary><p>58 pages, 11 figures, 37 tables</p></details> |
| **[Dissociating Answerability from Extraction in LLM Question Answering over Radiology Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17576v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17576v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Answering questions from radiology reports involves two decisions: whether the report contains an answer and which span gives it. We apply it to 13 LLMs on the RadQA test set, zero-shot and with three cumulative demonstration sets, the last adding an unanswerable example. Models with similar F1 differed more than twofold in false-answer rate. The unanswerable example changed when models answered, not how well they extracted: 12 of 13 models abstained more often, and three-quarters of the answers they withdrew had F1 $\geq$ 0.5. Within models, false answers showed no clear association with extraction quality, but acting on the decision itself helped: keeping an answer only when enough other models also answered lowered the zero-shot false-answer rate by 4 points without a detectable F1 change. | **[Parason: Revealing Subtask and Trial Parallelism in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24658v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.24658v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling test-time reasoning has substantially improved the problem-solving ability of large language models (LLMs), but standard autoregressive decoding still executes long reasoning traces sequentially, creating severe latency for difficult tasks (up to days and weeks). Parallel reasoning offers a natural remedy. However, prior systems primarily focus on Subtask Parallelism, where the model learns to decompose a high-level task into smaller chunks that can be solved independently. This approach overlooks another pervasive form of parallelism: Trial Parallelism, where multiple speculative attempts explore, verify, and aggregate competing hypotheses in parallel. In this paper, we introduce Parason, which reveals and learns both forms of parallelism in LLM reasoning. Our analysis identifies Trial Parallelism as the majority of parallelizable reasoning computation (65.5% in DeepSeek-V4's reasoning steps in HLE), and it becomes increasingly dominant on hard problems. Guided by this taxonomy, Parason converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories with a context-free grammar, then trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), whose reward jointly balances accuracy, latency, and the two parallelism ratios. At inference time, Parason executes the learned parallel structure through tool calls, translating theoretical savings to real-world wall-clock acceleration. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks including AIME24 and AIME25 show that Parason achieves an average acceleration about 1.7$\times$ while maintaining competitive accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[Many Steps into One: Reasoning Contraction and the Limits of Safe Memory Reuse for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17544v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17544v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Thought templates, skill libraries, agent memories and caches turn many reasoning steps into one. We call this reasoning contraction. A contracted memory saves reasoning steps and errors until its facts change, then quietly answers wrong. Most systems trust memories by counting successes and failures. We prove that such counters are badly tuned change detectors: a symmetric counter notices a partial change only after a power of its false-alarm horizon, and an unclipped one may never notice it. On five LLMs answering multi-hop questions over edited facts, ExpeL- and ACE-style counters serve 7.0-13.5x more stale answers than certified memories given the same information, and on code tasks with an exact verifier 35-73x more. Safety also has a price. When facts change and each memory is judged only by its own verified outcomes, one number, the certification load, sets how much reasoning any safe memory can skip, and under Zipf demand it yields a scaling law. Our recipe weighs each memory's failures by its own likelihood ratio, certifies its utility against live reasoning, and restarts the certificate when facts change, with the likelihood-ratio detector as the safety net for unannounced changes; for large edits it also invalidates the memory.</p></details> | 57 pages |

| **[Soohak: A Mathematician-Curated Benchmark for Evaluating Research-level Math Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09063v4)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.09063v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following the recent achievement of gold-medal performance on the IMO by frontier LLMs, the community is searching for the next meaningful and challenging target for measuring LLM reasoning. Notably, beyond standard problem solving, Soohak introduces a refusal subset that probes a capability intrinsic to research mathematics: recognizing ill-posed problems and pausing rather than producing confident but unjustified answers. | **[Towards Green AI: Evaluating Energy Efficiency for Quantized LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17531v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17531v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While it is known that quantization of large language models (LLMs) reduces memory usage via lower-bit weights, studies quantifying the resulting impact on energy usage and carbon intensity are scarce. We identify a novel trend showing that quantization techniques tend to exhibit peak energy efficiency relative to full-precision baselines when inputs are sufficiently long and outputs are short. Furthermore, we show that quantization strategies become marginally more energy-efficient relative to full-precision models as batch sizes increase, though gains are modest. Notably, fused-kernel implementations such as EETQ int8 and Bitsandbytes int8 offer the highest energy savings, up to 4x compared to FP32 on short text generation, with negligible accuracy loss. Finally, we observe that energy usage closely tracks runtime on our evaluated benchmarks, indicating that, in practice, latency optimization can serve as a reliable proxy for reducing the environmental footprint of LLM services. | **[Behavioral History Outperforms Descriptions of the Person for LLM Synthetic Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03998v2)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.03998v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as synthetic personas representing survey respondents. This aggregate similarity masks differences in variance: persona descriptions recover only 53-67% of human between-person variation, whereas adding behavioral history restores it to approximately the human level. At the individual level, description-based personas achieve only 7-12% of the informedness observed in human test-retest responses, while adding behavioral history raises this to 28%. | **[RogueMerge: Robust and Unified Attacks against LLM Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03344v2)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.03344v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model merging composes specialized capabilities into a single LLM by aggregating task-specific LLMs sourced from unverified public platforms, exposing a critical supply-chain attack surface: Because any malicious behavior can be encoded into a task vector, and merging grants third-party vectors direct write access to model weights, an attacker-provided task vector can enable or amplify diverse downstream threats. (ii) Attackers have no knowledge of the victim's merging configurations, causing a static attack vector optimized in isolation to be easily diluted or neutralized. (iii) Practical threat induction must generalize to attack prompts unseen during optimization, which static vectors cannot adequately encode. In light of these gaps, we present RogueMerge, the first unified attack framework addressing all three challenges. To handle autoregressive generation, we replace static arithmetic with a joint optimization that explicitly enforces attack success after merging. To better simulate unknown merging settings, we formulate attack injection as a stochastic min-max optimization problem and solve it via meta-gradient descent. To generalize across heterogeneous attack prompts, we employ distributionally robust optimization and derive a tractable first-order Taylor approximation at LLM scale, with a provable error bound. | **[SchemaWeaver: Accelerating On-Device Mobile Agentic AI via Schema-Guided Parallel LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17436v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17436v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) on mobile devices can act as agents that call external software tools to complete complex tasks, but the major challenge of supporting on-device mobile agentic AI with LLMs is the long latency of LLM tool calling on mobile devices. | **[VeriRouter: Towards Verifiable LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17431v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17431v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) routers are increasingly deployed to optimize quality, latency, and cost through dynamic model selection and request rewriting. | **[Reading the Room: Foundations, Design, and Challenges of Normative Competence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10906v2)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.10906v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human communities are governed by normative systems: shared standards that produce \textit{norms} dictating acceptable behavior, enforced through community sanctioning. Thus, alignment requires \textit{normative competence}: the ability to discern from interaction alone what norms a community enforces without relying on static pretrained knowledge. We introduce a multi-agent community debate setting, where access to debate is governed by synthetic norms, to study normative competence in isolation from pretraining exposure. We show that baseline LLM agents fail to learn norms even when doing so would improve their accuracy. We then experiment with various \textit{normative modules} -- architectural components for norm inference -- finding that norm-following is highly sensitive to both the style of norm and the model powering the normative module, suggesting a lack of generalizability. Furthermore, when idiosyncratic, non-normative behaviors accompany the true norm, LLM agents exhibit an unselective attribution failure: they indiscriminately copy idiosyncratic noise alongside enforced rules, a pattern that persists even when imitating unnecessary behaviors is explicitly penalized. | **[EPOCH: Coverage-Aware Evolution with Experiential Preference Optimization for LLM-based Automated Heuristic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17379v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17379v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based automated heuristic design (AHD) discovers heuristics for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs), where an LLM generates candidates in an evolutionary search. Since DPO only teaches the generator to rank these improvements, a reflective self-distillation (RSD) term makes it more likely to generate them on its own, and the gains accumulate over the long horizon of the search. | **[NeuroFilter: Activation-Based Guardrails for Privacy-Conscious LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14660v3)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2601.14660v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic Large Language Models (LLMs) are models able to reason, plan, and execute tools over unstructured data. | **[GPU Goldmines: Tunable WebGPU Kernels for Edge LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17321v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17321v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As demand for LLM inference grows and consumer GPUs grow more capable, edge deployment is becoming increasingly attractive. Shared evidence explains much of the observed association, the HotpotQA result disappears on hard questions, and correct and confidently wrong questions overlap. A monitor built from three partial runs predicts whole-trajectory variability (mean AUROC 0.81). Together, these contributions provide a measurement and evaluation framework for studying how agent trajectories stabilize and for testing when additional evidence, sampling, or review may be useful.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 16 figures. Camera-ready version, Workshop on Agent Behavior at COLM 2026</p></details> |
| **[TRAP: Tree-Guided Reinforcement-Augmented Probing for Automated LLM Jailbreaking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17313v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17313v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aligned large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to jailbreak prompts that circumvent their safety guardrails, making the identification of these vulnerabilities important for understanding and mitigating model weaknesses. Existing automated jailbreak methods explore prompt candidates or reuse attack experience, but generally do not learn over a persistent hierarchy that connects related jailbreak strategies, such as role-play, encoding, and obfuscation. This leaves the relationships among reusable strategies underused in guiding adaptive attack selection. We present Tree-Guided Reinforcement-Augmented Probing (TRAP), a hierarchical framework for automated jailbreak strategy discovery that requires only black-box access to the target LLM. TRAP organizes established jailbreak methods and successful attack templates into a semantic strategy tree, connecting broad attack approaches to concrete, reusable strategies. A reinforcement-learning policy based on a tree-structured gradient bandit explores this tree and uses evaluator feedback to update strategy preferences along each selected path. The policy retains these preferences across attack objectives, allowing experience from earlier attempts to guide subsequent strategy selection. Retrieval-augmented prompt construction translates the selected strategy into an attack for the current objective using relevant successful examples. Experiments on AdvBench across seven aligned LLMs show that TRAP outperforms its counterparts on six models under the reported method settings and the common primary evaluator, achieving attack success rates of 45.6% to 97.9% on these models. Code: https://github.com/Rezamirbagheri110/TRAP</p></details> |

| **[Visual Tokens on Demand: Visual Budgeting for Scalable and Efficient Multi-Image Reasoning in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18858v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18858v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models have advanced rapidly, but most remain optimized for single-image inputs. Multi-image reasoning is costly because each image adds many visual tokens, increasing encoding and attention demands. Existing techniques that address the above issue, including visual token pruning, token merging, visual token compression, learned resampling, hierarchical compression, retrieval-augmented visual reasoning, and memory-based architectures, have largely been designed for single-image scenarios, where redundancy is primarily spatial and local. Multi-image reasoning introduces additional challenges: redundancy may occur across images, task-relevant evidence may be sparse or distributed, and aggressive per-image compression can remove details needed for comparison, counting, temporal reasoning, retrieval, or evidence grounding. Moreover, existing token-reduction methods are often either (i) training-free but inference-inefficient, typically relying on accessing attention matrices to get token-importance that may be impractical to access with memory-efficient attention implementations such as FlashAttention, or (ii) training-dependent, requiring the base MLLM to be retrained or adapted to a new compressed-token paradigm. In this work, we study scalable multi-image reasoning and understanding without retraining the base model and without accessing attention matrices at inference time. To this end, we propose mmVB, a lightweight plug-and-play module that assigns query-conditioned per-image token budgets via cross-image context, adding only 1.6M trainable parameters while reducing visual input tokens by 9x. This reduction yields 5x faster time-to-first-token and 7x fewer prefill FLOPs, while maintaining 94.4% of full-resolution accuracy across eight multi-image reasoning benchmarks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bio-Inspired Electrotactile Feedback: A Framework and Impacts of Multimodal Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18746v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18746v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Electrotactile stimulation, as a haptic feedback method, often struggles to evoke high-quality natural sensations. Consequently, despite its practical advantages, it is generally less preferred in human-machine systems than mechanical approaches. In this paper, we propose a bio-inspired stimulation framework that generates signals modelled on afferent neural activity derived from a touch model. By producing more biologically plausible spiking patterns, the proposed approach aims to enhance the realism of perceived tactile sensations. To evaluate these stimulation patterns in a realistic task context and to quantify the influence of visual and movement cues, we conducted a series of subjective experiments under three conditions. The results indicate that bio-inspired modulation was effective: in particular, bio-inspired co-modulation of amplitude and frequency achieved a favourable pressure-irritation trade-off, increasing the probability of perceiving pressure while maintaining low irritation (pressure: 0.78; irritation: 0.24) relative to the other patterns. Visual cues and hand movement further improved feedback quality by significantly increasing the probability of reporting pressure (p$<$0.001) without a corresponding increase in irritation, and by increasing mean naturalness ratings. Overall, these findings demonstrate the potential of bio-inspired electrotactile patterns to improve haptic feedback quality, and highlight the importance of evaluating such methods under realistic, multimodal interaction scenarios.</p></details> |  |
| **[Context-aware Bayesian Fusion for Multimodal Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18741v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18741v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable detection of pedestrians and road users is essential for the safe operation of autonomous vehicles. However, this task becomes increasingly challenging under adverse environmental conditions, such as poor weather and low lighting, where the performance of state-of-the-art pre-trained object detectors and on-board sensors tends to degrade, which limits their effectiveness and reliability. Moreover, retraining models for each operating context imposes significant constraints due to high data requirements, computational resources, and time. Considering this, we propose a novel context-aware multimodal late fusion approach that integrates a lightweight gating network within our probabilistic calibration framework. The gating network extracts environmental context and dynamically modulates the detection outputs of pre-trained object detectors based on the inferred context, without retraining of the detectors. Our methodology is evaluated on a modified KITTI dataset, in which the camera images and lidar pointclouds are augmented for varying fog levels. Experimental results show that our approach yields an approximately 14% relative improvement over non-adaptive late fusion baselines, achieving a consistent average precision of 56% across different fog levels.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking Projector Training in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18598v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18598v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal LLMs are typically trained by first aligning a vision encoder with a language model via projector training on large image-caption datasets, followed by joint training of the full multimodal model. These results suggest that projector training mainly performs a coarse alignment, which many different projectors achieve equally well. Motivated by these findings, we propose PORTAL, a training-free projector initialization that requires neither paired image-caption data nor gradient-based optimization, but only per-modality summary statistics. PORTAL computes an optimal-transport-based map between Gaussian approximations of the vision and language feature distributions on a shared principal subspace, so that the projector training stage can be skipped entirely. Without any paired data, PORTAL matches the standard pipeline trained on 558k image-caption pairs across six LLM backbones, two vision encoders, and 16 benchmarks. | **[GeoMER: A Joint Euclidean-Hyperbolic Geometry Framework for Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18587v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18587v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) aims to understand human emotions by integrating information from multiple modalities such as speech and text. To mitigate this gap, we propose GeoMER, a joint Euclidean-Hyperbolic modeling framework that introduces hyperbolic emotion-core routing to capture higher-level, abstract emotional representations for MER. The proposed framework jointly models token-level cross-modal semantic interactions and latent emotion representations in two complementary geometric spaces. Specifically, GeoMER consists of a Euclidean Multimodal Fusion Module (EuclFusion) for multimodal representation fusion and a Hyperbolic Geometric Core Routing (HypGeoRoute) module that performs geometry-aware core assignment and aggregation in a hyperbolic manifold to capture more abstract feature representations. We experiment on the IEMOCAP multimodal MER benchmark and demonstrate that the proposed method achieves the best performance, with 83.54% weighted accuracy and 83.35% unweighted accuracy, outperforming Euclidean baselines, including MemoCMT.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ICONIP 2026 (33rd International Conference on Neural Information Processing)</p></details> |
| **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18447v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. The same parameterization supports multiple functions, including joint video-action generation, world prediction, policy inference, action-conditioned generation, video-to-action generation, and inverse dynamics. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. | **[Bifocal: Training-Free Mixed Granularity Visual Token Pruning for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18399v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18399v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual token pruning for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) appears highly effective, with existing methods retaining over 95% of unpruned performance on common VQA benchmarks using a quarter of the visual tokens or fewer. We trace these failures to two limitations of the prevailing paradigm of retaining the highest-ranked native patches. To address these limitations, we propose Bifocal, a training-free, two-stage pruning method built on a mixed-granularity framework. It allocates separate budgets to query-focused and image-intrinsic evidence, complemented by a pooled coarse representation of the entire image. Before the LLM, Bifocal matches question words to visual regions through a soft conjunction balanced against norm-debiased image saliency, while selecting image-intrinsic evidence to maximize representational coverage. | **[ReDiS: Reasoning-Diagnosis Self-Evolution for Multimodal Reasoning in Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18388v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18388v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown strong potential for remote sensing understanding, yet reliable reasoning remains challenging when tasks require fine-grained visual grounding and multi-step inference. | **[Let the Mind Conceive, Let the Hand Achieve: Following Multimodal Instructions for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18352v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18352v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot following a multi-step instruction needs to act on the right objects in the right order, even as the scene changes. | **[Where is the multimodal goal post? | **[Heading Hierarchy Reconstruction for Complex and Long Documents with Level-Wise Multimodal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18238v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18238v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) have been widely adopted for document parsing and optical character recognition (OCR), as they can accurately extract page-level elements. | **[Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Zero-Shot Human-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18161v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18161v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong zero-shot reasoning across diverse domains, but their potential for Human-Object Interaction (HOI) remains unexplored. | **[PolUR: Polarization Aware Multimodal Degradation Perception for Underwater Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17991v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17991v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recovering high-quality images from degraded underwater observations is crucial for underwater exploration. | **[MMTA: Benchmarking Multimodal Temporal Analysis with Time Series, Text, and Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17856v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17856v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series analysis is increasingly studied in multimodal settings, yet most benchmarks pair temporal signals with text or with visualizations derived from the same signal. | **[Silence and Noise: Resolving Suppression and Overvaluation in Multimodal Entity Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16106v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.16106v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal entity alignment (MMEA) is fundamental to integrating heterogeneous knowledge graphs (KGs), yet existing paradigms remain critically vulnerable to incomplete modalities and cross-graph structural asymmetries. | **[GesBench: When and Where Do Multimodal Language Models Perceive Social Meaning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15129v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.15129v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human social perception is incremental: we infer intent before an utterance is complete, revise our interpretation as new cues arrive, and attend to specific facial, gestural, or prosodic signals that shape social meaning. Across open and closed multimodal models, we find a clear human-model gap: humans form predictive understandings early and revise more often, while models often hedge or repeat across different clips. | **[Toward a Multimodal Foundation Model for Plasma State Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15076v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.15076v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Plasma experiments observe coupled physical states through sparse, heterogeneous, and incomplete diagnostics. | **[What Really Works for Universal Multimodal Embeddings? A Controlled Study of Contrastive Training Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15035v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.15035v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contrastive learning has become the standard paradigm for multimodal representation learning, aligning semantically matched pairs while separating mismatched ones in a shared embedding space. This motivates a fundamental question: Do sophisticated contrastive strategies consistently bring meaningful downstream gains in multimodal embedding learning? To address this, we systematically evaluate representative strategies under a unified benchmarking framework on MMEB. Surprisingly, direct evaluations reveal that most existing strategies offer marginal gains over a standard aligned baseline, with several even degrading overall performance. To uncover the underlying failure modes, we introduce an MLLM-as-a-Judge diagnostic framework that dissects top-1 retrieval behaviors into labeled positives, false negatives (unlabeled valid matches), and hard/easy negative errors. Our systematic analysis yields three key insights: (1) Gradient distortion: Upweighting gradients from high-similarity negatives consistently hurts downstream generalization; (2) Distribution illusion: Wider separation between positive and negative similarity distributions does not necessarily translate to better retrieval performance; and (3) Synergistic training: While false-negative mitigation or hard-batch construction yields limited gains in isolation, integrating hard-negative mining, false-negative mitigation, and hard-batch construction achieves optimal performance, delivering an average improvement of 6.0% on MMEB with Qwen2-VL-2B. | **[Prior-Fitted and Frozen Scorers for Multimodal Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14770v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14770v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal contrastive learning typically relies on manually specified scoring functions, such as cosine similarity, to quantify the similarity between representations from different modalities. | **[CUSE: Fine-Grained Multimodal Unlearning with Counterfactual Guidance and On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14763v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14763v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can reproduce sensitive, copyrighted, or unsafe information from their training data. | **[Are Multimodal Benchmarks Really Useful? Item-Level Multimodal Benchmark Diagnosis via Structure-Response Co-Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14542v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14542v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal benchmarks are central to evaluating whether models can reason over information from multiple modalities. | **[EVISTA: Unified Multimodal Video Search with Evidence-Aware Temporal Path Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14510v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14510v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In the era of big data, precise event retrieval from large video collections has become increasingly important and challenging. | **[Multi-agent Undercover Gaming: Hallucination Removal via Counterfactual Test for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11182v2)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2511.11182v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hallucination continues to pose a major obstacle in the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). | **[Timo: Taming Multimodal Diffusion Transformers for Human Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30761v2)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.30761v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most existing human motion generation (HMG) methods use cross-attention modules to inject text semantics, but ignore the importance of bidirectional modeling between motion and text tokens, which limits text comprehension. | **[Design Choices for Multimodal Transformer Learning with Missing and Longitudinal Healthcare Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14436v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14436v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning offers the potential to improve predictions by integrating complementary information from heterogeneous data sources. | **[Scaling Laws for Multimodal Data Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14423v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14423v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier AI systems are increasingly natively multimodal, jointly pretrained on multiple modalities, such as text, vision, and audio. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) increasingly adopt Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, as MoEs scale efficiently and partially limit cross-modal interference. Second, repeating vision or audio data beyond 2x yields negligible benefits. Finally, cross-modal interactions are highly asymmetric: vision strongly interferes with audio, whereas audio's interference with learning text and vision is negligible. | **[Clinical value claims in multimodal AI outpace supporting evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14395v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14395v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal prediction models add a data source, such as imaging or an assay, to clinical information, and health systems must decide whether that added test is worth acquiring. | **[V-ICAL Bench: Evaluating Video In-Context Learning for Multimodal Agents in Interactive Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15683v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.15683v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While In-Context Learning (ICL) enables models to adapt from exemplars without parameter updates, multimodal ICL remains largely underexplored, particularly regarding video demonstrations in interactive environments. | **[Long-MDR: Long-Context Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Deep-Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05195v2)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.05195v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The next generation of multimodal research agents must reason over long-lived research histories rather than short model completions.
