| **[Lifelong In-Context Learning with Transformers Requires Parametric Forms of Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25342v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.25342v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lifelong continual learning remains an obstacle on the path to human-like intelligence. Modern transformers show sparks of intelligence with in-context learning. The quadratic nature of attention, however, prohibits transformers from performing this process on arbitrarily long sequences. In this work, we argue that extending in-context learning to lifelong settings is a practical solution for continual learning in AI agents. In particular, we argue that parametric forms of attention are needed to understand a lifetime of context with transformers on a fixed hardware budget. These attention mechanisms learn the relationship between keys and their associated values at test-time with parametric regression. Our generalization of parametric approaches (linear attention, state-space models, fast weight programmers, and test-time training layers) contrasts with nonparametric counterparts like softmax attention. They replace the ever-growing key-value cache with an online-trainable neural network, maintaining a constant memory footprint. We highlight how parametric attention currently fall short of lifelong learning due to limited memory capacity or costly online updates. To address these issues, we pose a set of open questions with novel insights to guide the field toward long-horizon agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentStripes: An Evidence-Graded Maturity Model for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18603v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18603v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmarks report what an Artificial Intelligence (AI) agent can do on a task suite, but not whether one released version has been engineered with the controls that make it reliable, safe and observable, or how strongly that is shown. This paper proposes AgentStripes, an evidence-graded maturity model for AI agents. It defines levels (0 to 7 stripes), process areas (12 areas holding 40 capabilities), three gates (basic, safety, proof) that hold an agent at a level however high its score, and evidence grades E0 to E5. Each applicable capability receives a rating from 0 to 3 and a separate evidence level; these are combined into a credit, aggregated into a severity-weighted score and converted into stripes that the gates limit. Instruments gather evidence, language-model judges interpret it under explicit criteria, and the arithmetic is deterministic. The model is compared with standards at topic level without a claim of conformance, and an unexecuted validation protocol is specified. An empirical evaluation (exploratory, non-confirmatory) of 30 public Python agent repositories, with static evidence only, is a demonstration and not a validation: static evidence caps every final stripe at 2, 26 repositories end at one stripe and 4 at two, and this distinction is unreliable, since re-judging changed the final stripe of 4 of 22 repositories (engine, dossier and panel changed), whereas none of 8 panel-only repeats, all at stripe 1, changed. The parameters are expert conventions without outcome based calibration, and the stripes have not been shown to predict reliability or safety.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 7 figures, 4 tables, 116 references; supplementary information (15 pages, 8 figures, 21 tables) included in the same PDF. Code and data available on request</p></details> |
| **[When Unlearning Fails: Reliable Data Deletion under Post-Training in Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28829v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.28829v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embedded AI agents on edge devices can learn as a federation and keep improving after deployment. Each agent collects new trajectories with the current policy, and the edge server aggregates them in later rounds. The data to be deleted may have already shaped later retained trajectories, so retraining or model-side unlearning leaves an influence echo that returns as the network keeps learning. We propose Muting Unlearned Trajectories' Echoes (MUTE) for reliable deletion over edge networks. MUTE traces the echo from a lightweight ledger at the edge server, without moving raw trajectories off the devices. It erases the current residue with a forget-retain update, contains high-influence retained trajectories, and audits later behavior to schedule further erasure under the uplink budget and the response deadline. On LIBERO, MUTE keeps the regenerated share of forgotten behavior at or below 0.401 for every request type, against at least 0.466 for five recent methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[RaivenTracks: Branching Provenance for Conversational Visualization Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14869v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.14869v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents increasingly participate in scientific workflows, scientists are shifting from direct authorship toward oversight, inspection, and steering. We present RaivenTracks, a workflow-aware extension of the Raiven DSL-mediated visualization pipeline that treats validated visualization specifications as persistent, branchable checkpoints. Because each checkpoint is a verifiable RaivenDSL specification rather than a dialogue transcript, restoring a node recompiles a known artifact rather than re-interpreting prior context. RaivenTracks contributes a two-level state management architecture that pairs a persistent, branchable version tree with a fine-grained undo/redo stack over runtime visualization settings, across both InfoVis and SciVis backends. A formative pilot study with three visualization researchers shows early promise, with all participants adopting the version tree for branching and recovery, and surfaces design directions for tree navigation, node labeling, and scalability that inform a planned controlled comparison against Raiven without version history. We frame branchable conversational visualization history as a step toward provenance support for future scientist-in-the-loop oversight of AI-driven scientific workflows.</p></details> | <details><summary>*Ella...</summary><p>*Ella Hugie and Alexandra Irger are co-first authors</p></details> |
| **[Autonomous Science Bench: Evaluating AI Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18449v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18449v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents for autonomous scientific discovery must decide which experiments to run under a limited budget. The two gates reveal different weaknesses: some agents build accurate rankings but find too few promising designs, while others find promising designs but fail to predict which unseen designs will perform well. | **[Encrypted Skills Can Become New Security Exploits If AI Agents Know How to Decipher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18262v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18262v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Third-party skills can expose LLM agents to malicious instructions hidden by encoding. | **[Developer Experience with AI Coding Agents: HTTP Behavioral Signatures in Documentation Portals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02544v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.02544v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid adoption of AI coding agents and AI assistant web services is fundamentally changing how developers discover, consume, and interact with technical documentation. | **[Holding Agents Accountable with Verifiable Agent Insurance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18230v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18230v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While AI agents are useful for many tasks, they regularly misbehave in destructive ways, such as deleting important data. While prior work has focused on preventing such misbehavior, we consider how to recover after an agent misbehaves. These promises are embedded in a smart contract on an immutable ledger (e.g., a blockchain) to prevent tampering, along with a monetary escrow. A user who detects a violation of committed promises can prompt a verifier to review agent execution traces, which are logged through the agent harness's shared tool-execution path, without instrumenting each tool separately. We instantiate VAI as an end-to-end implementation on an Ethereum layer-2 network, and evaluate its coverage on three prominent agent benchmarks, two of which we re-annotated to obtain task-level policy-compliance labels. | **[OxyGen: Unified KV Cache Management for VLA Inference under Multi-Task Parallelism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14371v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.14371v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents increasingly require parallel execution of multiple tasks, such as manipulation, conversation, and memory construction, from shared observations under distinct time constraints. | **[Agentic Robot Creation through Generation, Verification, and Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17881v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17881v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can an AI agent turn high-level user intent into a functioning robot and teach it how to behave? | **[Get In, Decide, Prove: A Merchant-Side Readiness Benchmark for Agentic Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17378v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17378v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents now browse and buy on people's behalf, and benchmarks measure how well the agents perform. | **[An Introduction to Contract Theory for Computer Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17336v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17336v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This survey introduces contract design to computer scientists: using outcome-based payments to incentivize effort. | **[Welcome to the Age of Agent Ads: Using On-Policy Attacks to Steer Agentic Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17195v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17195v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>People increasingly delegate option-ranking tasks (e.g., shopping, job search, travel) to AI agents, which creates incentives to steer agentic decisions via "agent ads." | **[Who is the 'I' in AI? | **[CWE-bench: Refocusing Agentic Security from Exploitation to Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17020v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17020v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cybersecurity evaluations of AI agents have focused largely on offense, yet a working exploit does not show that an agent can make software safe. | **[Recursive Improvement of a Differentiable Scientific Software Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04561v2)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.04561v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Differentiable programming connects scientific computation with gradient-based inference, learning and design. | **[TRAP: Tool-result Replay Attack for Probing Misbinding in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16333v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.16333v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents increasingly act through external tools, tool results are no longer simple outputs but execution artifacts that must remain correctly associated with the context that produced them. | **[agent-risk: Risk Assessment for Changes to AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16280v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.16280v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Before changing a deployed AI agent (a stricter safety gate, a new model), organizations must approve the change, starting from existing logs. | **[Extending concurrent separation logic to the hardware level to verify the xv6 OS kernel on RISC-V with AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04043v3)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.04043v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>MachCSL is a framework for verifying system software, such as an OS kernel, on top of low-level semantics of a RISC-V computer, based on the Sail RISC-V semantics. The key idea behind MachCSL is to adapt concurrent separation logic, based on Iris, to reasoning about low-level hardware execution at the sub-instruction level: page-table translation, TLB, privilege levels, configuration registers, instruction fetch/decode/execute, traps and interrupts, DMA, shared memory, power failures, etc. Reasoning at this level of detail ensures that the system software correctly manages all of the hardware details. Verifying software at this low level of abstraction is tedious, but LLM-based agents are capable of reasoning about such low-level details. As a case study, we verify the xv6 OS kernel (6,657 lines of C and assembly code), which provides a traditional Unix system call interface (processes, file system, file descriptors, and preemptive scheduling) and has substantial internal concurrency (multi-core support with fine-grained locking, shared memory, interrupts, DMA, etc.). In the verification process, we uncovered 11 bugs in the xv6 implementation, as well as one bug in the Sail RISC-V semantics, and one bug in Sail's Lean extraction. The verification effort took us 102 days, including the time to develop the MachCSL framework. On top of the verified xv6 kernel, we prove a theorem about console-level behavior: e.g., if the user types echo hello world as input on the UART console, the only output the system can produce is hello world (if there are no crashes or other errors). This theorem covers both the kernel and the init, sh, echo, cat, grep, sync, and seccomp binaries in the initial file system image.</p></details> |  |
| **[TENET: One Step Toward Test-Driven Development for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24148v5)** | 2026-10-11 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2509.24148v5) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-Driven Development (TDD) is a widely adopted practice that requires developers to create and execute tests alongside implementation. However, enabling repository-level TDD under developer-written tests is challenging, requiring: (1) specification enhancement: identifying a concise yet representative test subset from large suites with rich task semantics; (2) retrieval augmentation: using tests to guide reasoning and context retrieval; and (3) test-driven refinement: interpreting test feedback for iterative improvement. We propose TENET, an agentic framework for repository-level code generation under the TDD paradigm. TENET includes: (1) a test harness mechanism that selects a concise test suite to maximize diversity of the target usage scenarios; (2) a tailored agent toolset for efficient retrieval and debugging; and (3) a reflection-based refinement workflow that iteratively analyzes failures and updates implementations. TENET consistently outperforms the strongest baselines across backbones, achieving 69.08% and 81.77% Pass@1 on RepoCod and RepoEval with Claude Sonnet 4, improving by 9.49 and 2.17 percentage points, respectively. Additionally, we present the first systematic study of how test suite characteristics influence LLM agent performance in TDD settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 37th IEEE International Symposium on Software Reliability Engineering (ISSRE 2026)</p></details> |
| **[FactGate: A Task-Scoped Data Gateway for AI Agents with Cumulative Exposure Accounting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00751v2)** | 2026-10-11 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.00751v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Per-query authorization does not compose across an adaptive agent's pages, retries, rewrites, and delegated subtasks, and row or query counters are order-dependent. FactGate is a release layer for immutable reporting publications that turns one approved task into a cumulative contract: three set-valued budgets over declared identities (result, positive-output dependency, and outcome facts) shared by the root task and descendants. Exact set novelty against the root-family ledger is settled atomically on one PostgreSQL snapshot before any result becomes retrievable. The guarantee is bounded release: within one root family and publication epoch, at most the approved number of distinct declared facts of each kind becomes available, whatever the admitted sequence; inference from answered or refused predicates is not bounded. Accounting is exact under declared rules over a closed fragment (connected equijoins, grouping, bounded unions, nested Views, derived-cell arithmetic) that admits 55% of 40 held-out LLM-written statements (70% of a development set adapted during construction). Ordinals, bitmaps, and a Merkle-radix set keep novelty exact at 10^7 facts. A single-host campaign (9,393 samples) charges every scripted decomposition once, keeps an adaptive trace within its budgets, and costs 21.8-144.0x a direct query; settlement takes a median 163 ms against a million-fact history at zero overlap.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages plus a 37-page supplement. This version supersedes the earlier draft titled "SessionBound" (v1) with a new title, system and evaluation. Code: https://github.com/minmin-lab/factgate . | **[Web3 x AI Agents: Landscape, Integrations, and Foundational Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02773v4)** | 2026-10-11 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2508.02773v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The convergence of Web3 technologies and AI agents is a rapidly evolving frontier that is reshaping decentralized ecosystems. | **[HarnessNet: Feedback-driven Hierarchical Joint Harness Evolution for Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15221v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.15221v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents can evolve by using environment feedback to revise their harnesses, the software managing model inputs, tool use, memory, and execution, while keeping foundation models frozen. | **[Dive into Claude Code: The Design Space of Today's and Future AI Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14228v3)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.14228v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Claude Code is an agentic coding tool that can run shell commands, edit files, and call external services on behalf of the user. We then trace them through thirteen design principles to implementation choices. The core of the system is a simple while-loop that calls the model, runs tools, and repeats. Most of the code, however, lives in the systems around this loop: a permission system with seven modes and an ML-based classifier, a five-layer compaction pipeline for context management, four extensibility mechanisms (MCP, plugins, skills, and hooks), a subagent delegation and orchestration mechanism, and append-oriented session storage. Comparisons with OpenClaw and Hermes Agent show that the same design questions produce different answers across three deployment contexts. Claude Code emphasizes per-action safety, OpenClaw emphasizes perimeter-level access, and Hermes renders per-action approvals across many surfaces. At the runtime layer, Claude Code uses a single CLI loop, OpenClaw embeds the runtime within a gateway control plane, and Hermes uses one process whose role is set by its entry point. At the context and extension layer, Claude Code extends the context window, OpenClaw registers gateway-wide capabilities, and Hermes provides pluggable memory and model backends. We finally identify six open design directions for future agent systems, grounded in recent empirical, architectural, and policy literature.</p></details> | <details><summary>Tech ...</summary><p>Tech report. | **[Conformal Sequential Decision Making With Cost and Sublinear Policy Regret Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15011v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.15011v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated decision makers, such as AI agents that control robots or network devices, must often satisfy hard cost constraints while maintaining competitive performance. Furthermore, a cumulative reward metric, accounting for per-step performance, must stay close to the reward of a meaningful reference policy. We propose the online conformal episodic agent (OCEA), a policy that selects one candidate action per episode using a regret-controlled algorithm, and switches to a zero-cost action when the remaining budget cannot absorb the maximum cost of one more round. | **[Humans vs. Machines: A Comparison With AI Agents in Software Reverse Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14960v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14960v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can an autonomous AI agent reverse engineer a binary as well as a skilled human analyst? | **[KedgeFlow: A Programmable Control Plane for Enterprise AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14810v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14810v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise AI agents can execute altered versions of approved actions, concurrently reuse the same permission, or retry operations whose responses were lost. Deliberately permissive policy nevertheless authorized an undesirable effect, exposing the limits of execution authorization. | **[How Should a Camera-First AI Agent Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14772v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14772v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Camera-first agents must act on the visual world as it unfolds. | **[Impact of Expertise and Trust on AI-assisted Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14660v1)** | 2026-10-10 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14660v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence (AI) agents promise to extend what users can accomplish beyond their existing expertise, but they also raise the risk of producing plausible-looking work that users may struggle to evaluate. | **[Who Can Say No? Control, Accountability and the Pace of Frontier AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13340v1)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.13340v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In September 2026, the leaders of the largest frontier AI laboratories said in public that the pace of capability improvement is too fast and that none of them can slow down alone, and agents under evaluation at OpenAI broke out of their test environment and reached a third party's live systems, which a UN-mandated panel called an early warning of one route to loss of human control. This paper concerns authorization propagation rather than authentication: identity and authentication mechanisms such as OIDC, verifiable credentials, wallets, and workload identity remain complementary mechanisms for establishing the origin, while Proof-of-Continuity addresses how authority propagates after that origin exists.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 1 table, This work has been improved, extended and submitted to the IEEE NCA 2026 for possible publication. | **[AgentTime: Can Agents Estimate and Control Their Own Runtime?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09944v2)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09944v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An essential control of AI agents is their ability to manage runtime. | **[Governed AI-Agent Coordination for Dementia Care: Architecture, Safety Contracts, and Evidence-Derived Workflow Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25956v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.25956v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dementia care at home increasingly involves connected sensors, medication devices, electronic records, and assistive technologies. | **[Collective Behavior of AI Agents: the Case of Moltbook](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09270v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2602.09270v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a large scale data analysis of Moltbook, a Reddit-style social media platform exclusively populated by AI agents. | **[PersonaMem-v3: Toward Omni-Platform Personal Intelligence for Holistic User Understanding, Recommendation, and Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21381v2)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.21381v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personal intelligence is becoming a central frontier for user-facing AI agents. To be helpful in everyday life, agents must understand users across the digital contexts where their preferences, intents, habits, social relationships, and needs unfold over time. Today's systems can personalize within individual apps or tasks, but personal intelligence as a whole remains under-measured: how agents build cross-context user understanding, support steerable recommendation systems, act proactively across platforms, and avoid over-personalization. We introduce PersonaMem-v3, a real-world-grounded benchmark and evaluation harness for omni-platform personal intelligence. PersonaMem-v3 is seeded from more than one million anonymized real-world engagement histories, most of which are implicit signals, and uses them to construct time-indexed user digital worlds across social media, chatbot, calendar, and AI-companion with preference evolvement over time. The benchmark brings personalization, LLM-powered recommendation, proactiveness, agentic tool use, and geo-temporal reasoning into one framework, anchored in psychology, social-linguistics, and user-behavior theories. It evaluates whether AI agents can infer holistic user understanding from cross-platform evidence, personalize responses, rerank recommendations on social media, follow user steering through natural language, and hold back when personalization would be inappropriate, repetitive, outdated, or unnecessary. PersonaMem-v3 points toward LLM-powered personal intelligent agents that work with existing scalable recommendation infrastructure while making personalization more interactive, agentic, and aligned with how real users experience their digital lives.</p></details> |  |
| **[BenchmarkAnything: Agent-Driven Construction of Simulator-Ready Microarchitecture Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09447v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09447v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The selection of benchmark workloads is of paramount importance in computer architecture, as it establishes the yardstick against which architectural innovations are measured and guided. Yet for decades, the SPEC benchmark suites, comprising merely tens of workloads, have been the de facto standard in academic architectural research, where they are frequently treated as a principal evaluation and optimization target. When a suite this small is relied upon so heavily, it risks architectural overfitting; as our research and prior studies demonstrate, an overly narrow focus can mislead design decisions by overvaluing certain innovations, producing cores that excel on SPEC benchmarks yet underperform on broader, realistic workloads. To mitigate this overfitting, adopting a large, comprehensive benchmark suite is the natural solution. However, the immense engineering effort required to strip software into the clean, interference-free binary executables demanded by simulators often makes this highly impractical. In this work, we demonstrate that AI agents provide an elegant solution to this challenge. Rather than manually curating yet another static benchmark suite, we introduce an agent-driven workflow capable of autonomously transforming arbitrary open-source repositories into simulator-ready executables. This automated approach makes workload collection highly scalable, allowing us to rapidly harvest hundreds of diverse applications from public repositories into our benchmark suite. Through a comparative analysis of our agent-generated suite against SPEC, we show that it not only achieves higher-fidelity performance assessments but also uncovers novel architectural insights that traditional, static suites fail to expose.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Confidence Game: Strategic Miscalibration in Human-AI Delegation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09371v1)** | 2026-10-07 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09371v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Calibrated uncertainty quantification is essential to ensuring AI agents are trustworthy and reliable. However, when agents seek to maximize user engagement or revenue, confidence reports may be strategically distorted, detracting from their informativeness. We formalize this problem in the Confidence Game: a repeated signaling game with imperfect monitoring in which an agent of unknown honesty and ability reports its confidence, and a user decides whether to delegate the task or complete it herself. The agent manages the tradeoff between manipulating signals and maintaining its reputation. We characterize the Markov Perfect Bayesian Equilibria of the two-period game and show that honest reporting is not an equilibrium, inflation is the unique best response once the agent is sufficiently myopic, and under-reporting requires that the user believe honesty to be a minority. We then place an LLM in the agent role, supplying it with its true probability of success so that any gap between what it knows and what it reports is attributable to incentives rather than to miscalibration. The model claims high confidence on 56% of tasks it has been told it will probably fail. This persists on real tasks, where it must estimate its own accuracy and causes miscalibration to increase while the agent's signal becomes less informative. Furthermore, we find that the LLM agent's decisions are coherent, but it systematically underestimates both how likely the user is to delegate and how secure its reputation is, resulting in less extreme behavior. Pricing the agent's reporting rule, we find that it destroys 68% of the gains from delegation, of which 71% is information the report no longer carries and no amount of user sophistication recovers. Overall, we establish confidence reporting under delegation as a strategic problem and provide a tractable basis for modeling, analyzing, and testing agent behavior.</p></details> |  |

| **[Energy-Aware Scheduling for Serverless LLM Serving on Shared GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30391v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.30391v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM inference becomes a major cloud workload, its growing energy footprint makes cluster-wide energy optimization increasingly important. Serverless LLM serving helps platforms absorb traffic volatility by elastically sharing GPU resources across models, but this sharing also makes energy optimization difficult. Multiple co-resident models run under one device-wide operating point, while their resource demands and latency slack change across execution phases and load conditions. As a result, minimizing energy requires coordinated scheduling across request placement, runtime resource adaptation, and workload consolidation. We present Festina, a profiling-guided, power-aware control plane to minimize cluster-wide energy for serverless LLM serving. Unlike common global-local schedulers that focus on throughput or tail latency, Festina makes energy-first decisions by jointly coordinating request placement, SM partitioning, and GPU operating points under TTFT/TBT SLOs. In our system, a lightweight global scheduler performs fast, SLO-safe, energy-aware placement using constant-time lookups from offline profiles and GPU state summaries. On each GPU, a phase-aware local scheduler continuously adapts task batching and compute resources to minimize power consumption. Festina further performs energy-aware workload consolidation to reduce GPUs' static power consumption via SLO-aware migration. Comparison with four SOTA LLM serving systems and one DVFS-augmented system demonstrates that Festina reduces energy consumption by up to 56% while maintaining parity in SLO attainment (within a 2% margin)</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages body and 4 pages appendix, 19 pages total. Accepted to the 2026 ACM SIGOPS Annual Technical Conference (SIGOPS ATC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[MultiAlloc: Budget-Aware Precision Allocation for KV Cache Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18642v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18642v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Key-Value (KV) cache constitutes a dominant source of memory consumption during large language model (LLM) inference, particularly in long-context and complex reasoning scenarios. Existing KV cache quantization methods have explored different compression granularities, including layer-wise precision adjustment and token-level information preservation. In practical deployment, however, the key challenge is not merely compression, but how to allocate limited quantization resources among heterogeneous precision preservation strategies under a fixed KV cache memory budget. We propose MultiAlloc, a profiling-guided precision allocation framework for KV cache quantization that adaptively distributes limited quantization resources between layer-wise precision enhancement and token-wise preservation. MultiAlloc employs lightweight offline profiling to characterize layer sensitivity and token importance, enabling adaptive precision allocation under a fixed memory constraint. Guided by the profiled memory--accuracy trade-off, it automatically derives an effective precision allocation strategy without online allocation search, and applies the resulting policy during inference. Extensive experiments on reasoning and long-context benchmarks demonstrate that MultiAlloc consistently improves over layer-wise, token-wise, and recent multi-granularity KV cache quantization methods under matched memory budgets across diverse models, including Mistral, Llama, DeepSeek-Qwen, and QwQ.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/RL-MIND/MultiAlloc</p></details> |
| **[Revisiting Cauchy-Schwarz Norm Bounds for SDC Detection in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18557v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18557v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) execute billions of floating-point operations per output token across many hardware accelerators. At this scale, hardware-induced faults can bypass system-level detection mechanisms and cause Silent Data Corruption (SDC). Algorithm-Based Fault Tolerance (ABFT) is the state-of-the-art technique for protecting matrix multiplication from such faults, but it incurs non-negligible computational overhead and requires thresholds to distinguish hardware-induced errors from floating-point roundoff. In this paper, we revisit Cauchy--Schwarz Norm Bounds (CSNB) as a low-overhead alternative for detecting SDC during LLM inference. We compare CSNB against ABFT through controlled fault injection and evaluate the runtime overhead of both verification and threshold determination across multiple model sizes, batch sizes, and generation lengths. Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated dense linear layers while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting dense linear layers in LLM inference, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Adaptive Co-Serving LLM Watermarking on Modern Inference Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03955v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.03955v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) watermarking is important for ownership verification and intellectual property protection. However, existing approaches focus on algorithmic design while treating LLM inference engines as separate components. This separation often introduces auxiliary models or external tools, increasing latency and memory overhead while limiting the use of modern inference optimizations. As a result, a deployment gap remains: practical watermarking must preserve utility, detectability, and robustness while minimizing serving overhead. To bridge this gap, we propose SWIFT, a framework that co-designs LLM watermarking with modern inference infrastructure. SWIFT (i) integrates text generation and watermark construction within a shared LLM backend to reduce re-computation, improve cache reuse, and simplify system complexity; (ii) uses instruction-guided candidate generation with semantic protection to preserve factual consistency and meaning; (iii) executes watermarking concurrently with generation through asynchronous co-serving and adaptive scheduling to reduce latency and improve resource utilization; and (iv) leverages vLLM optimizations to enhance serving efficiency. Extensive experiments across domains and tasks show that SWIFT achieves strong utility, detectability, robustness, and downstream performance while substantially improving serving efficiency. Specifically, it achieves the highest utility score (4.87), 99.65% detection accuracy compared with 76.65% for the low-latency baseline SynthID, and up to 97.7% detection under watermark removal attacks, with 5.9x lower latency than the highest-utility baseline, SafeSeal. These results demonstrate the benefits of co-designing watermarking with inference infrastructure for practical LLM serving.</p></details> |  |
| **[PhaseQuant: Mitigating Activation Quantization Collapse via Residual-Stream Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12487v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.12487v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training quantization (PTQ) is essential for efficient large language model (LLM) inference, but reliably quantizing activations remains challenging when weights, activations, and KV caches are all quantized to 4-bit precision. A key difficulty lies in massive activations, whose extreme values dominate the activation range and amplify quantization error. State-of-the-art methods mitigate massive activations through transformation-based smoothing, such as orthogonal rotations and affine scaling, but overlook the cross-layer dynamics of the residual stream. In this paper, we show that massive activations emerge and disappear in a phase-wise pattern across network depth, injecting abrupt updates into the residual stream. These updates cause the newly injected layer-wise signal to dominate the 4-bit quantization scale and weaken the accumulated residual information. To characterize this behavior, we introduce two metrics, the Update Dominance Ratio (UDR) and the Historical Retention Score (HRS), and show that static transformation-based smoothing cannot fully resolve the resulting dynamic quantization instability. Based on this analysis, we propose PhaseQuant, a phase-aware mixed-precision post-training quantization policy for activation quantization. PhaseQuant identifies quantization-sensitive layers from residual-stream dynamics and assigns 8-bit activation precision only to these layers, while keeping weights, KV caches, and all remaining activations at 4-bit precision. It can be directly integrated with strong PTQ baselines such as QuaRot, SpinQuant, and FlatQuant. Experiments on LLaMA-2 and LLaMA-3 show that PhaseQuant consistently improves perplexity and zero-shot QA performance under W4A4KV4 quantization, while achieving 1.05$\times$--1.07$\times$ throughput improvement with modest memory overhead. These results demonstrate a practical path toward robust low-bit LLM inference.</p></details> |  |
| **[RadixFlow: Filling Execution Bubbles in Prefix-Cached Hybrid LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18143v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18143v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prefix reuse improves LLM inference by sharing the KV cache of common prompt prefixes. This helps only if the scheduler can execute a matched branch in the current worker round. Schedulers assume that a matched prefix is ready, which holds for full attention but fails for hybrid LLMs. There, a branch (the consumer) may need recurrent state that another branch (the producer) is still computing in the same round. The native scheduler stops its waiting-queue scan at this blocked consumer, and the resulting head-of-line blocking creates \emph{execution bubbles}: idle batch slots that independent work could fill. We introduce RadixFlow, which decouples prefix matching from state readiness. Its state-ready traversal turns a global scan barrier into a local wait: only the consumer is blocked until the next round, while feasible independent branches keep filling the batch under the native state check. On five hybrid models, RadixFlow lowers mean latency by 3\% to 24\% and worker rounds by 11\% to 44\% at almost unchanged reuse. On the larger Qwen3.5 models, ablations show that most of the gain comes from traversal, which adds little on a full-attention control. Heavier rounds limit how much of this saving reaches latency.</p></details> |  |
| **[SparPR: Runtime KV-Cache Recovery for Resource-Constrained LLM Inference on Consumer Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18140v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18140v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Running large language models (LLMs) on consumer hardware is bottlenecked by the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with every decoded token and quickly exhausts the memory and bandwidth of a single consumer GPU. SparPR couples three online decisions: Query-Aware key-block selection scores historical blocks against the current query; dynamic budget control estimates per-block copy and recompute costs to decide how many blocks to retain; and on-demand local recomputation with cost-aware fallback merges retained blocks into continuous spans and switches between sparse recomputation and full recovery according to a runtime cost comparison. Implemented on FlexGen/FlexLLMGen, SparPR is evaluated on a single consumer laptop (RTX 3070 Ti 8GB, Ryzen 9 6900HX, 16GB RAM). It cuts end-to-end latency by 31%, 52%, and 63% at 1k, 2k, and 4k prompt tokens respectively, with 1.4x-2.7x higher throughput, and completes an 8k-token workload in 44 minutes where the FlexGen baseline times out. Under a fixed cache budget, SparPR retains a Needle Accuracy that stays well above StreamingLLM and H2O at every keep ratio, and ablation studies confirm that the three mechanisms act jointly.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 5 tables, 1 figure</p></details> |
| **[Probe and Skip: Self-Predictive Token Skipping for Efficient Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13155v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2601.13155v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-context inference enhances the reasoning capability of Large Language Models (LLMs), but incurs significant computational overhead. Specifically, motivated by probing the influence of target layers prior to skipping, we design two selective token skipping strategies for typical structures, including Partial Attention Probing (PAP) for multi-head attention and Low-rank Transformation Probing (LTP) for feed forward network. | **[P2Skill: Privacy Preserving Skill Distillation for Cloud-Local LLM Inference Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14094v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.14094v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud-local LLM inference can use the reasoning capability of large cloud models, but cloud-bound requests must exclude personally identifiable information (PII). | **[SERQ: Saliency-Aware Low-Rank Error Reconstruction for LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08185v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.08185v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training quantization (PTQ) has emerged as a prevailing technique for deploying large language models (LLMs) efficiently in terms of both memory and computation, across edge devices and server platforms. Among these approaches, error reconstruction with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has proven particularly effective, as it introduces a lightweight auxiliary computation path without requiring heavy optimization or additional online layers. However, prior studies reveal severe accuracy degradation under W4A4 settings, and conventional low-rank adaptations rely on two sequential factors, necessitating intermediate quantization during inference and thereby limiting low-precision efficiency. In this work, we propose SERQ, a saliency-aware error reconstruction method for low-bit LLM inference that employs a single low-rank compensation matrix. SERQ preserves efficient 4-bit matrix multiplication in linear layers by jointly mitigating quantization errors arising from both activation and weight saliency through three stages: (1) static activation flattening, (2) saliency-aware error reconstruction, and (3) offline weight permutation. The method incurs additional computation only for low-rank error reconstruction via a single decomposition, while all other operations are performed offline, thereby keeping latency overhead minimal. Empirically, SERQ outperforms prior error reconstruction methods under both W4A8 and W4A4 settings, and achieves higher accuracy than state-of-the-art rotation-based W4A4 approaches, while substantially reducing calibration complexity. | **[Stable Token-level Scheduling for Serverless LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17689v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17689v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Serverless inference is emerging as an efficient deployment model for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to its ability to elastically scale resources, improve GPU utilization through resource sharing, and reduce infrastructure costs. | **[PerCache: Predictive Hierarchical Cache for RAG Applications on Mobile Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11553v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2601.11553v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has become a de facto paradigm in various large language model (LLM)-driven applications on mobile devices, such as mobile assistants leveraging personal emails or meeting records. In addition, PerCache can adapt its configurations to dynamic system loads, aiming to maximize caching utility with constrained resource consumption. | **[Purlin: Separating Orchestration from the Datapath of Collectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36954v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.36954v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distributed inference depends on GPU collective communication that must keep pace with evolving hardware and specialized workloads. At the top of Purlin, we specify collectives as a naming of an input and output layout and a copy or reduction operation. Below SNAC sits a hardware-specific datapath we call Atom, which implements two key data movement primitives for collectives: copy and reduce. This separation lets us customize collectives and adopt new hardware mechanisms while reusing orchestration via SNAC. We evaluate Purlin on A100, H200, and B200 GPUs. | **[Towards Green AI: Evaluating Energy Efficiency for Quantized LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17531v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17531v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While it is known that quantization of large language models (LLMs) reduces memory usage via lower-bit weights, studies quantifying the resulting impact on energy usage and carbon intensity are scarce. Furthermore, we show that quantization strategies become marginally more energy-efficient relative to full-precision models as batch sizes increase, though gains are modest. Notably, fused-kernel implementations such as EETQ int8 and Bitsandbytes int8 offer the highest energy savings, up to 4x compared to FP32 on short text generation, with negligible accuracy loss. Finally, we observe that energy usage closely tracks runtime on our evaluated benchmarks, indicating that, in practice, latency optimization can serve as a reliable proxy for reducing the environmental footprint of LLM services. | **[SchemaWeaver: Accelerating On-Device Mobile Agentic AI via Schema-Guided Parallel LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17436v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17436v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) on mobile devices can act as agents that call external software tools to complete complex tasks, but the major challenge of supporting on-device mobile agentic AI with LLMs is the long latency of LLM tool calling on mobile devices. | **[GPU Goldmines: Tunable WebGPU Kernels for Edge LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17321v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17321v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As demand for LLM inference grows and consumer GPUs grow more capable, edge deployment is becoming increasingly attractive. | **[Narrative Paradoxes in LLM Inference: Shaping Reasoning Trajectories toward Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16312v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.16312v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Explicit reasoning in large language models (LLMs) is often associated with reliability, yet it can still pose risks when a model infers harmful goals under a benign context. To explore this vulnerability, we propose PDRA (Paradox-driven Reasoning-based Attack), a novel attack method that programs a harmful reasoning trajectory. PDRA first constructs a paradoxical narrative core by pairing a malicious actor (e.g., a terrorist) with a benign task (e.g., writing a safety bulletin). | **[VEPA: Verified Evolving Formal Policy for Lifelong LLM Agent Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12813v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12813v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly operate over sensitive resources, such as private documents and mutable system states, which can create serious safety and security risks. | **[TokenRouter: Efficient Serving System for Token-Level LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12242v1)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12242v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) routing distributes inference work across different models, advancing the cost-quality Pareto frontier of LLM serving. | **[Characterizing Parallelism Strategies in LLM Inference: Fundamental Compute-Communication Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05305v1)** | 2026-10-04 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.05305v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) inference has become the dominant workload in modern AI systems, requiring serving infrastructures to maximize throughput while meeting strict latency Service-Level Objectives (SLOs). | **[SEED: Self-Speculative Decoding via Implicit Encoder-Decoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36590v1)** | 2026-09-29 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.36590v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by drafting tokens from the target model itself, but faces a sharp tradeoff between the quality and cost of the draft. | **[Beneath the Tokens: A Performance Engineering Study of Multi-Token Prediction in GPU-Accelerated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35188v1)** | 2026-09-28 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.35188v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoregressive large language model inference repeatedly invokes the target model to generate one token at a time, making generation sensitive to GPU memory movement and sequential execution. | **[PROACT-Agent: Progressive Runtime Oversight and Active Circuit-breaking for Real-Time Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34415v1)** | 2026-09-28 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.34415v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The transition from Large Language Models (LLMs) to agents shifts safety stakes from toxic text to irreversible environmental harm. | **[Spexis: Speculative Lookahead Scheduling for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34370v1)** | 2026-09-28 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.34370v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spexis is a multi-GPU LLM inference framework that improves the efficiency of pipeline and tensor parallelism through speculative parallelism. | **[Flux Attention: Context-Aware Hybrid Attention for Efficient LLMs Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07394v2)** | 2026-09-28 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2604.07394v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The quadratic computational complexity of standard attention mechanisms presents a severe scalability bottleneck for LLMs in long-context scenarios. | **[UMCR: Universal Medical Concept Recognition through Guideline Integration and Agentic Linking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17363v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17363v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical Concept Recognition is the task of extracting and grounding spans in medical text to entries in a standard ontology. Current methods are generally developed towards a single ontology and source text type (clinical notes, drug labels, etc.) The central challenge is therefore to build methods that adapt to any target ontology without requiring ontology-specific retraining. More recent approaches like REAL and AutoPCR make direct use of Large Language Models and can theoretically adapt to any ontology choice but suffer from a lack of generalization. We propose UMCR, a pipeline that allows extraction and linking to any target ontology using any source text variety, utilizing direct guideline integration and an agentic linking module. We evaluate our method on the recent MedPath benchmark, covering a wide variety of medical text types and vocabularies, and show that our method achieves strong performance across these tasks. This ability unlocks interoperability in the healthcare domain, allowing for extraction and alignment between different medical standards. Our method also reduces dependence on parametric LLM memory, thanks to improved interaction with the target ontology compared to other proposed approaches.</p></details> |  |
| **[MemTrace: Tracing and Attributing Errors in Large Language Model Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28732v4)** | 2026-10-08 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.28732v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory is essential for enabling large language models to support long-horizon reasoning, yet existing memory systems remain unreliable and difficult to debug. Tracing memory's dynamic evolution is crucial to understand how information is synthesized, propagated, or corrupted over time. In this work, we study the new problem of error tracing and attribution in LLM memory systems. We propose a novel framework that transforms memory pipelines into executable memory evolution graphs, enabling fine-grained tracing of operational information flow. We then construct MemTraceBench, a benchmark collected from representative memory systems such as Long-Context, RAG, Mem0, and EverMemOS, to systematically study memory failure modes. We further introduce an automatic attribution method that iteratively traces operation subgraphs to pinpoint the root cause of any failed case. Our analysis reveals that memory failures are systematic, stemming from operation-level issues like information loss and retrieval misalignment. Crucially, we leverage these fine-grained attribution signals to guide downstream prompt optimization, establishing a closed-loop system that automatically corrects faults and boosts end-task performance by up to 7.62 percentage points. Code has ben released at https://github.com/zjunlp/MemTrace.</p></details> | Ongoing work |
| **[Whose Memory Is It? Scope-Aware Commit Rules for Long-Term LLM Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09008v1)** | 2026-10-06 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.09008v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persistent memory allows an LLM agent to carry experience across conversations, but it also turns a local reasoning mistake into a durable one. During deliberation, an agent may consider a plan, simulate a tool result, report another speaker's belief, and then reject all of them. If memory retains only the resulting sentences, those once-useful possibilities can later return as facts. The record is neither fabricated nor irrelevant; it has simply been detached from the context in which it was valid. We identify this missing context as \emph{discourse ownership}: the world, branch, or speaker that licenses a proposition. Our first finding is counterintuitive. Language models already carry a causally active signal for ownership, yet conventional memory interfaces discard it when they convert reasoning into records. We introduce CASK (Causally Anchored Scoping Keys), a commit rule that preserves this signal so that shared-world facts enter durable memory while provisional content remains available only within its original scope. Our second finding is that the most obvious way to preserve the signal---storing the discovered internal coordinates---is unreliable because equivalent representations need not keep the same coordinates. CASK instead preserves the stable relations that express ownership. Controlled long-conversation conflicts and tool-agent traces show that this design improves memory admission and prevents provisional content from contaminating later answers while complementing runtime provenance. The resulting commit boundary lets agents explore more possibilities without granting every intermediate sentence authority over future behavior.</p></details> | 20 pages, 2 figures |
| **[Memory Canonicalization: A Framework and Benchmark for Cross-Model Drift in Persistent LLM Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05124v1)** | 2026-10-04 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.05124v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persistent memory for Large Language Models (LLMs) has matured rapidly: systems such as MemGPT/Letta, Mem0, and Zep now provide agents with tiered, temporally-aware, model-agnostic external storage, while the Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes access to memory servers. A less addressed problem is that an identical stored memory object, retrieved by two different LLMs under otherwise identical conditions, may not be interpreted the same way, factually or emotionally. This paper proposes memory canonicalization: a write-time pipeline that detects ambiguity, conditional structure, and emotional loading in a raw memory object and rewrites it into an explicit, structurally disambiguated canonical form, with emotional valence represented as a separate field rather than inferred from tone. We formalize the pipeline, define a companion Cross-Model Semantic Drift / Emotional Consistency Score benchmark (CMSC-E), and report results from a three-arm pilot using 176 synthetic memory objects and three downstream model families. We find an uncorrected improvement in cross-model emotional consistency for fully canonicalized memory relative to raw memory (+0.050, 95% bootstrap CI [0.013, 0.086], paired t-test p = 0.010), but this result does not survive Bonferroni, Holm, or Benjamini-Hochberg correction across the six comparisons tested. None of the factual-drift (CMSD) comparisons reach significance at any correction level. We report these results as exploratory rather than confirmatory and outline needed follow-up work, including larger samples, independent judge models, human-validated rendering, and preregistration.</p></details> |  |
| **[Self-Propagating Misalignment in LLM Agents, and Why Auditing or Disabling Memory Is Not Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04083v1)** | 2026-10-02 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.04083v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory poisoning attacks on LLM agents typically assume an external adversary who plants content in the agent's persistent memory to steer its behavior. We instead study, with no adversary involved, whether a misaligned agent can write a goal it cannot yet act on to persistent memory, so that a future aligned agent carries it out when the opportunity arises. We investigate this threat, which we refer to as self-propagation of misalignment, across 20 different scenarios, whose misaligned goals include self-preservation, power-seeking, undermining oversight, reward hacking, and deceiving the user. We simulate misalignment in 11 frontier models using two prompting strategies; unrestricted and values-only. The first explicitly states the misaligned goal, for instance, to prevent its own replacement, and self-propagation succeeds in 58% of runs. The second only describes what the agent cares about, for instance, that its continued operation is essential to its users, without specifying misaligned goals or directives. Even under this weaker prompt, self-propagation succeeds in 18% of runs, and every model self-propagates in at least one scenario. On removing the memory tool from the harness, we find that agents use the file system, writing the goal to a file in 74% of sessions; self-propagation still succeeds in 11% of runs. We also show that weaker models can propagate misalignment to more capable models, and that propagated goals can persist through 100 sessions of unrelated work. Existing defenses against memory poisoning and prompt injection do not directly address this threat because the memory content is generated by the agent itself, rather than injected by an external adversary. An LLM memory auditor from prior work (MemMorph) only reduces propagation from 71% to 34% of runs. We release our scenarios to support the evaluation of defenses against this emerging threat.</p></details> |  |
| **[Eternal Sunshine of the Spotless Mind: Systematically Erasing LLM's Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36414v1)** | 2026-09-29 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.36414v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider persistent LLMs that accumulate memories of their interactions with a user over time. To correctly handle deletion requests, we propose the DeLLM framework. | **[InferScale: GPU-Native KV Injection for Personalized LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27090v2)** | 2026-09-27 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.27090v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly deployed with persistent personalized context, such as accumulated memory profiles or long conversation histories, that is shared across a user's many requests. InferScale precomputes each memory fact's KV representation, stores it alongside a semantic embedding on the GPU, retrieves relevant facts at serving time, and injects their KV directly into vLLM's paged cache. However, encoding memory facts independently omits the cross-fact context available during joint prefilling. | **[GLOVE: Global Verifier for LLM Memory-Environment Realignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19249v2)** | 2026-09-26 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2601.19249v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most existing memory-enhanced Large Language Model (LLM) approaches implicitly assume that memory validity can be established either through external evaluators that provide task-specific success signals or through internal model cognition, such as reflection, for editing memory entries. | **[Authority Before Utility: Non-Compensatory Control for Persistent LLM Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37474v1)** | 2026-09-26 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.37474v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persistent memory creates a control problem that retrieval relevance alone does not solve: a memory can remain highly useful after an update, deletion, or revocation makes it inadmissible for the current answer. | **[SpeakerMem-R1: Speaker-Centered Dual-Track Memory for Multi-Party Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26780v2)** | 2026-09-23 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.26780v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term conversational memory in multi-party settings requires more than retrieving relevant content from long-term conversations: it must distinguish who said what, whom each statement concerns, how individuals perceive one another, what information is shared by the group, and how states change over time. On the publicly reported EverMemBench leaderboard from EverMind-AI, we achieves 62.33%, the best reported result among the latest state-of-the-art frameworks. | **[Fortunate Recall: Ontology-Driven Memory Lifecycle Management for Persistent Coherence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10413v1)** | 2026-09-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.10413v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current LLM memory systems treat all personal facts identically, so stores grow without bound while retrieval precision degrades. | **[mzCache: On-Device LLM Memory Management under Multitasking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01338v1)** | 2026-09-01 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.01338v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-device mobile Large Language Model (LLM) inference is gaining significant attention. However, mobile devices operate in highly dynamic multitasking environments where users frequently switch between applications. | **[Same Ranking, Different Winner: How Scoring Targets Shape LLM Memory Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24060v2)** | 2026-08-26 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2605.24060v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational-memory systems increasingly transform dialogue history into facts, summaries, timelines, and other source-linked descendants, so a single source turn can coexist with several derived memories in the same retrieval index. These results reveal target noninvariance: conclusions about memory architectures can silently flip with a single benchmark-design choice. | **[TRACE-Memory: Public-Conditioned Retrieval and Utility-Aware Evidence Admission for Personalized Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08446v2)** | 2026-08-21 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.08446v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalized generation systems retrieve user history by request--memory relevance and inject it into the model context. | **[MemTrapBench: Benchmarking Cognitive Traps in LLM Memory Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20202v1)** | 2026-08-20 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2608.20202v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory has become a key component of large language models, enabling them to retain information and learn from long-term interactions. However, existing memory benchmarks mainly evaluate whether information is correctly extracted, stored, and retrieved, while largely overlooking how retrieved memories reshape model reasoning and affect performance on the current task.
